Hydrogen storage potential of cubic InXH3 (X = Be, Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba, Ra) hydride perovskites: a comprehensive first principles investigation
Authors: Abu Bakr Amin, Hamza Naeem, Muhammad Rizwan, Zahid Usman, Yangwei Wang
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Safe and efficient solid state hydrogen storage remains a major challenge for practical hydrogen energy systems, particularly in achieving thermodynamic stability with favorable release characteristics. In this work, the hydrogen storage potential of cubic hydride perovskites InXH3 (X = Be, Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba, Ra) is systematically investigated using density functional theory within the CASTEP framework. Structural optimization confirms stabilization in the cubic Pm-3 m phase with tolerance factors ranging from 0.8450 to 1.0884, indicating geometric suitability for hydrogen incorporation. Elastic constants satisfy mechanical stability criteria, and bulk modulus decreases from 25.55 GPa for InMgH3 to 14.09 GPa for InRaH3, revealing tunable lattice stiffness that can influence hydrogen release behavior. Electronic structure analysis shows metallic character for InBeH3 and InMgH3, while InCaH3, InSrH3, and InBaH3 exhibit direct band gap semiconducting nature with HSE06 band gaps of 0.574 eV, 0.496 eV, and 1.045 eV, supporting stable bonding environments. Optical response across 0–35 eV confirms robust dielectric and absorption characteristics. All compounds possess negative formation energies, confirming thermodynamic feasibility. InBeH3 exhibits the highest gravimetric capacity of 2.38 wt% and volumetric capacity of 57.15gH2/L. Estimated desorption temperatures for all hydrides exceed room temperature and increase with atomic mass, indicating controlled hydrogen release suitable for off board hydrogen storage applications.

Introduction

Population growth and the rapid expansion of industrial sites have more than quadrupled energy consumption over the past decades. Rapidly increasing fossil fuel demand has a significant impact on environmental sustainability. Because increasing carbon dioxide emissions from fossil fuel use directly impact ecosystems and organismal communities, it is well recognized that carbon dioxide emissions contribute to global warming, primarily from fossil fuel combustion. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CO}_{2}$$\end{document} Production has risen from 20.7 Giga tons to 32.5 Giga tons over the years, with a documented 35% rate of emissions linked to the combustion of carbon-based fuels and the use of less efficient fossil fuels1. Extensive use of fossil fuels has led to significant CO2 emissions, contributing to the greenhouse effect and global warming2.

Due to increasing demand for fossil fuels and environmental pollution, there is a need for economically viable and energy-efficient resources. Hydrogen as a fuel can meet these requirements, producing environmentally benign byproducts such as H2O vapor, while also delivering a high energy output3. Among the most promising sustainable energy sources, hydrogen is considered superior to traditional fossil fuels such as coal, oil, and natural gas4. Hydrogen has a chemical energy density of 142 MJ/kg, exceeding that of fuels such as coal and hydrocarbons. It is lighter and more efficient, with a gravimetric energy density of 33.3 kWh/g, compared with gasoline’s 12.2 kWh/g. Despite its low volumetric energy density of approximately 3 Wh/L, hydrogen is a significant and reliable energy source, particularly because of its compatibility with fuel cells and its higher efficiency (60%) compared with diesel (45%) and gasoline (22%)5,6. Therefore, Hydrogen is becoming an increasingly important material worldwide as a viable option for clean, efficient energy1. A hydrogen economy that encompasses production, storage, and consumption could be part of future energy scenarios due to the characteristics of hydrogen2. Many techniques exist for producing hydrogen, such as photocatalytic water splitting7, traditional methods such as steam reforming natural gas, partial oxidation of hydrocarbons, and coal gasification. In contrast, the most essential techniques for producing hydrogen are based on renewable energy sources like wind, hydropower, photolysis, and photovoltaic electrolysis8. The main objective of this research is to store hydrogen by overcoming some of the interception challenges.

The first challenging perspective is that hydrogen requires more space to store energy because it has low volumetric energy density6. The second difficulty is that it can be used only in high-pressure, high-temperature environments. The third difficulty is that the loading and unloading procedures result in low adsorption and desorption rates, which may affect the efficiency of hydrogen storage9. Chemical hydrogen storage is the fourth challenge. Hydrogen is released during the chemical reaction that occurs in the storage container. However, the overall complexity of the system and the regeneration process can be challenging. Metal hydride storage was the fifth difficulty we encountered when storing hydrogen. When metal reacts with hydrogen, the hydrogen is stored within the lattice structure that forms as a result of the interaction10,11.

Hydride perovskites with the general formula ABH3 are increasingly studied as solid state hydrogen storage materials because of their high hydrogen content and tunable thermodynamic stability. Recent first principles studies have evaluated their structural stability, phonon behavior, elastic properties, and hydrogen storage metrics. For example, NaXH3 (X = Ga, In) systems were reported to exhibit favorable formation energies and mechanical stability12. Similar DFT investigations on XInH3 (X = Li, K)13 and (X = Rb, Cs)14 confirmed stable perovskite phases with promising hydrogen storage capacities. Zr-based XZrH3 hydrides also showed competitive stability and storage characteristics15, while LiBH3 type16 and MgXH3 (X = Cr, Fe, Mn)17 perovskite hydrides demonstrated stable elastic behavior and moderate dehydrogenation energetics. Broader computational screening further supports perovskite hydrides as viable hydrogen release candidates18, and experimental work on NaMgH3 validates the structural feasibility of this class19. These studies collectively establish hydride perovskites as a promising platform for solid state hydrogen storage.

Perovskite hydrides and oxy-hydrides have emerged as multifunctional mixed anion materials in which hydride anions modify electronic structure, defect chemistry, and transport behavior. Pure hydride ion conduction was first demonstrated in layered perovskite oxy-hydrides20, and subsequent work confirmed highly correlated hydride diffusion and the critical role of anion ordering in enhancing conductivity21,22. These materials have also been developed as hydrogen permeable electrodes with coupled hydride and electronic transport23. Beyond ionics, lattice hydride in BaTiO3−xHx type systems has been shown to directly participate in catalytic hydrogenation and to improve water tolerance of supported metal catalysts24. Rare earth oxy-hydride thin films exhibit reversible photo-chromism linked to defect redistribution and structural evolution25. In addition, hydrogen rich perovskite type hydrides have been experimentally explored as superconductors under moderate pressure26. Together, these studies demonstrate that perovskite hydrides span hydride ion conduction, hydrogen transport, catalysis, photo-responsive functionality, and superconductivity.

Ali et al.27 reported hydrogen storage potential of Cs2LiGaH6 and Cs2LiInH6 by employing GGA-PBE approach and find that both compounds possess cubic structures, negative formation energies and thermodynamic stability. They have gravimetric capacities of 1.73 wt% and 1.53 wt%, and volumetric densities of 71.6 and 63.5 g H2/L. both of these compounds showed more than DOE targets. Li et al.28 reported that Ti0.75Zr0.3–xCr0.75Mn1.25Nbx alloys with Nb substituted for Zr showed improved wide temperature hydrogen storage. Ti0.75Zr0.22Cr0.75Mn1.25Nb0.08 delivered 1.7 wt% at 25 °C, while Ti0.75Zr0.20Cr0.75Mn1.25Nb0.10 achieved 1.71 wt% at – 20 °C with 95.49% capacity retention after 200 cycles. Nb weakened metal hydrogen bonding, reduced hysteresis, and enhanced desorption performance. A first principles DFT study on a hydride perovskite done by Boubchir et al.29 that confirmed thermodynamic and dynamic stability with half metallic behavior. The Rb based 5d compounds showed gravimetric hydrogen capacities of 1.133 wt%, 1.121 wt%, and 1.111 wt% for Ta, W, and Re, respectively, indicating potential for hydrogen storage applications.

The current study focuses on the study of structural, elastic, electronic, optical properties and hydrogen storage potentiality of alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}. Cambridge Serial Total Energy Package CASTEP code was utilized in this analysis and within DFT framework, GGA-PBE was employed for analyzing alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}\left(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra\right).$$\end{document}.

Computational approach

The first principal computation is important for designing novel material applications since it predicts outcomes only based on theoretical information. Several DFT-based computer platforms are appropriate for theoretical research, including the Cambridge Serial Total Energy Package (CASTEP), Wien2K, VASP, and Quantum ESPRESSO. In this study, DFT based module of Material Studio, Cambridge Serial Total Energy Package (CASTEP) was utilized in order to estimate the structural, optical, elastic, and electronic properties of alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskite hydrides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}30,31.

The mechanical, electrical, and optical properties of cubic perovskite hydrides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}\left(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra\right)$$\end{document} based on alkaline earth metals were investigated using the plane-wave pseudo-potential approach combined with Generalized Gradient Approximation (GGA) and Perdew–Burke–Ernzerhof (PBE)31,32. This technique allows for faster calculations while preserving efficiency and assuming orbital shape. The nucleus and inner electrons are assumed to operate as an ion core with which valence electrons can interact in order to deliver well-converged results and a fast convergence of the electron-ion potential33. The electronic configurations of In ([Kr] 4d105s25p1), Be (1s²2s²), Mg ([Ne] 3s²), Ca ([Ar] 4s²), Sr ([Kr] 5s2), Ba ([Xe] 6s2), Ra ([Rn] 7s2), and H (1s1)10. Electronic wave functions can be described using a plane-wave basis with a cutoff energy of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$400eV$$\end{document}, which differentiates core and valence states and prevents charge spillage from core states. To investigate the impact of alkaline earth metal atoms on cubic perovskites hydrides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}\left(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra\right)$$\end{document}, all simulations were performed using a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\times1\times1$$\end{document} supercell (Fig. 1). The Monkhorst-pack approach was used to sample irreducible Brillouin zones over a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6\times6\times6$$\end{document} k-point grid. Geometry is optimized for the lowest feasible energy, known as the ground state energy of the crystal structure, to maintain structural stability. To solve the Kohn-Sham equations iteratively, the Self Consistent energy tolerance was set to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\times{10}^{-5}eV/atom.$$\end{document} The maximum displacement and applied stress were set at\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.002 \mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.1GPa$$\end{document}, accordingly, while Hellman–Feynman forces per atom were set at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.05eV/\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ$$\end{document} to reduce the stresses10,31,33.

Alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskite hydrides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} possess a cubic crystal structure with a space group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Pm-3m(\#221)$$\end{document} and the atoms are at Wyckoff position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1a:In(0,0,0),2b:X(0.5,0.5,0.5)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3c:X=H(0.5,0.5,0)$$\end{document}10. Many characteristics, including lattice parameters and electronic band gaps, as well as optical, mechanical, and electronic attributes of different alkaline earth metals based hydride perovskites, can be manipulated.

Results and discussion

Structural properties

Figure 1 demonstrates the structure of alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}, with Indium occurring at vertices, while the central alkaline earth metal cation X2+ forming an octahedral site, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}-atoms pointing at the centers of the six cube faces. The geometry optimization of alkaline earth metals-based cubic perovskites: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3},InMg{H}_{3},$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3},InSr{H}_{3},InBa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} was done by employing GGA-PBE approximation. Within the cubic phase and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Pm-3m(\#221)$$\end{document} space group, the lattice constant with unit cell Volume were calculated as illustrated in Fig. 3H. The initial phase defines the optimal lattice parameter values for stabilizing the crystal lattice of cubic perovskites. The lattice constants raised as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3.79\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ,4.00\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ,4.39\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ,4.5\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ,5.04\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ,$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5.20\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3},InMg{H}_{3},$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3},InSr{H}_{3},InBa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} respectively. This elevation in “a” is the cause of transition from Be to Ra consecutively. This is due to the fact that as Shannon’s ionic radii34 of alkaline earth metals is of the increasing order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Be\left(0.45\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)<Mg\left(0.72\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)<Ca\left(1.00\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)<Sr\left(1.32\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)<Ba\left(1.35\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)<Ra\left(1.62\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)$$\end{document} while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}^{-}\left(1.46\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${In}^{3+}\left(1.48\mathop {\rm{A}}\limits^ \circ\right)$$\end{document}. Goldschmidt tolerance factor35, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$"{t}_{f}"$$\end{document} estimated the structural stability and given by mathematical relation.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}_{f}=\frac{({r}_{In}+{r}_{{H}^{-}})}{\sqrt{2}({r}_{{X}^{2+}}+{r}_{{H}^{-}})}$$\end{document}

As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}_{f}$$\end{document}assess’s the stability of perovskites and ranging from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.8-1$$\end{document}. Hence, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${t}_{f}>0.8$$\end{document} for all perovskites\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}, as illustrated in the Table 1. Goldschmidt tolerance factor revealed that all perovskite structures exhibited excellent structural stability36,37.

Mechanical stability

Many physical characteristics of materials determine their specific industrial applications based on mechanical stability. Flexibility, rigidity, durability, strength, stiffness, and other qualities can all be used to evaluate the extent of a material’s mechanical robustness. Hence, elastic constants like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{ij}({C}_{11}$$\end{document},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{12},{C}_{44})$$\end{document} in Fig. 3A–C governed the mechanical stability. These constants have a significant impact on the nature of materials’ binding capabilities, elasticity, mechanical stability, and fragility. As according to Born-Huang criteria, the resultant outcomes proclaimed that alkaline earth metal based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} is stable as:31,40.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{C}}_{11}>0,{C}_{44}>0,({C}_{11}-{C}_{12})>0,({C}_{11}+{2C}_{12})>0,\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{C}12<\mathrm{B}<\mathrm{C}11$$\end{document}

Bulk modulus (B), Shear modulus (G), Young’s modulus (E), Cauchy’s pressure (C′′), Kleinman parameter (ζ), Poisson’s ratio (v), Pugh’s ratio and Shear anisotropic factor (A) are calculated by using elastic constants\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{ij}({C}_{11}$$\end{document},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{12},{C}_{44})$$\end{document}. Shear modulus41,42 is a mathematical term that describes how susceptible it is to shape change without altering the volume of crystal.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{H}=\frac{{\mathrm{G}}_{v}+{\mathrm{G}}_{R}}{2}$$\end{document}

Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{v}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{R}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{H}$$\end{document} are indicting viogt, reuss, and hill approximations accordingly and are given by10.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{v}=\frac{{C}_{11}-{C}_{12}+3{C}_{44}}{5}$$\end{document}5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{G}}_{R}=\frac{{5(C}_{11}-{C}_{12}){C}_{44}}{3\left({C}_{11}-{C}_{12}\right)+4{C}_{44}}$$\end{document}

The bulk modulus determines crystal stiffness and is the ratio of stress to strain41,42.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{B}}_{H}=\frac{{B}_{R}+{B}_{v}}{2}$$\end{document}

Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{B}}_{v}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{B}}_{R}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{B}}_{H}$$\end{document} represent viogt, reuss, and hill approximations respectively and are given by7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${B}_{R}={B}_{v}=\frac{{C}_{11}+{2C}_{12}}{3}$$\end{document}

The bulk modulus (B) expresses how resilient a material towards deformation. The bulk modulus (B) of the material declines when we move consecutively from Be to Ra. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InMg{H}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} are the compounds that best withstand volumetric changes under pressure10,36,37,43.

Poisson’s ratio41,42 is an important tool for calculating the mechanical features of crystalline solids.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=\frac{3B-G}{2(3B+G)}$$\end{document}

Materials with a Poisson’s ratio of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.26$$\end{document}have strong mechanical stability as shown in Fig. 3D, as predicted by theory. High Poisson’s ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu>0.26)$$\end{document} suggests strong ionic bonding, which enhances the material’s resistance to structural deformation. A low Poisson’s ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\nu<0.26)$$\end{document} implies covalent bonding, which is often linked to brittleness10. As elaborated in Table 1, the Poisson’s ratio of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InSr{H}_{3}\left(0.3152\right),InBa{H}_{3}\left(0.3175\right),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}\left(0.3374\right)$$\end{document} revealed ionic bonding while, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}\left(0.2569\right),InMg{H}_{3}\left(0.22\right),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3}\left(0.2909\right)$$\end{document} revealed covalent nature of alkaline earth metal based cubic perovskites.

Pugh’s ratio \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left(\rho\right)$$\end{document} is used to measure a material’s ductility or brittleness as in Fig. 3E. It is the ratio of the bulk modulus to the shear modulus.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho=\frac{B}{G}$$\end{document}

The material will be ductile, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho>1.75$$\end{document}, possesses high plasticity and is mechanically valuable while, the material will be brittle if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho<1.75$$\end{document}, and is fragile under applied stress10,31,44.

Mathematically, Cauchy’s Pressure can be expressed as10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{C}}^{{\prime}{\prime}}={C}_{12}-{C}_{44}$$\end{document}

Mechanically, it serves as a foundation for describing materials. Using this mathematical relation, one may quickly estimate a material’s brittleness and ductility43. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathrm{C}}^{{\prime}{\prime}}<0$$\end{document} in Fig. 3F for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}, therefore, all perovskite hydrides are brittle44.

The Shear Anisotropic Factor (A) (Fig. 3G) provides insights into the directional dependability of elastic properties and material strength and resilience. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=1$$\end{document}, the material exhibits isotropic behavior, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\ne1$$\end{document} implies anisotropy10. Mathematically, it can be written as;11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\frac{2{C}_{44}}{{C}_{11}-{C}_{12}}$$\end{document}

Young’s Modulus (E)41,42, often known as the modulus of elasticity, is a measurement of a material’s resistance to stretching or compression along its length45.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=\frac{9GB}{3B+G}$$\end{document}

Kleinman Parameter is represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\upzeta}$$\end{document} and can be expressed mathematically:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\upzeta}=\frac{{C}_{11}+8{C}_{12}}{{7C}_{11}+2{C}_{12}}$$\end{document}

It is a dimensionless parameter with values ranging from zero to one. If values are lower or near 0, it suggests a limited contribution of bond bending, whereas 1 indicates that bond stretching is feasible owing to applied external pressure31. As shown in Fig. 4(A-D), Young’s modulus, linear compressibility, Shear modulus, and Poisson’s ratio are depicted. All of these diagrams are made with the Elastic Tensor Analysis ELATE code46. All of the above-mentioned parameters are elaborated in Table 1.

Machinability is an important factor that governs a material’s ability to be formed with cutting tools. The machinability index (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mu}_{M}$$\end{document}) is critical in identifying the best amount of machine usage, cutting forces, temperature, and power strain for economical machining of a material. Mathematically, it can be written as the ratio of Bulk Modulus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left(B\right)$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{44}$$\end{document}47,48.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mu}_{M}=\frac{B}{{C}_{44}}$$\end{document}

Electronic properties

The electronic band structure and density of states (DOS) of alkaline earth metal based cubic perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} play critical roles when estimating their electrical properties. These properties suggest whether the materials are metallic, semiconducting, or insulating, as well as the types of bonding between their individual elements and band gap energies. The band structure provides critical information about the material’s conductive qualities and forms a link between crystal structure and electronic/optical properties. Studying electronic properties is critical for having an in-depth knowledge of these perovskites49. DFT investigations utilizing the GGA-PBE functional can determine the band structure of alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} with high symmetry points X, R, M, G, and R in the first Brillouin zone. Figure 4A–F shows the predicted band structure with Density of States (DOS) for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Electronic structure plays a central role in determining hydrogen storage behavior because it controls how strongly hydrogen binds to the host material. The density of states near the Fermi level influences charge transfer between hydrogen and the surrounding metal atoms. When hydrogen 1s orbitals strongly hybridize with metal d or p states, the bonding becomes more covalent. This typically increases structural stability but also raises the energy required for hydrogen release, which can limit practical storage performance50,51. On the other hand, materials with more ionic metal-hydrogen interactions tend to bind hydrogen less strongly. This can lower desorption temperature and improve reversibility, although excessively weak bonding may reduce storage stability52. Therefore, achieving an appropriate balance between covalent and ionic character is essential for effective hydrogen storage. Band gap characteristics also provide useful insight. Wide band gap materials often exhibit localized bonding and stronger hydrogen stabilization, whereas metallic or narrow band gap systems allow easier electronic redistribution, which may support hydrogen diffusion and reduce kinetic barriers53. In hydride perovskites specifically, the degree of orbital overlap between hydrogen and metal states has been directly linked to hydrogen release energetics and overall thermodynamic stability18. Together, electronic descriptors such as band gap magnitude, density of states near the Fermi level, and orbital hybridization patterns offer valuable guidance for predicting hydrogen storage performance from first principles calculations.

Fig. 4Computed band structures accompanied by DOS by utilization of GGA-PBE exchange correlation functional for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Computed band structures accompanied by DOS by utilization of GGA-PBE exchange correlation functional for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Figure 4A–F illustrates band structure along with DOS while the dotted red colored line indicating EF (fermi energy level) which is set at zero and is exploited as a reference line. The obtained band structure and DOS are investigated within the energy range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-15to15eV$$\end{document}.

Fig. 5Band structures calculated using Hybrid functional HSE06 for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Band structures calculated using Hybrid functional HSE06 for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Following a clarification of the band structure, the potential of electron-occupied bands and forbidden energy gaps are investigated. The bandgap \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({E}_{g}\right)$$\end{document} is a clear distinction between Valance band maxima (VB) and Conduction Band minima (CB). Bandgaps are classified into two categories: the direct and indirect bandgap. Indirect electronic transitions occur when the Valance band maxima (VB) and Conduction Band minima (CB) fail to line up at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k=0)$$\end{document}, and electronic transitions occur in conjunction with the generation of phonons. A portion of the energy may be wasted during indirect electronic transitions. Another scenario is direct band gap, in which the Valance band maxima (VB) and Conduction Band minima (CB) have the correct alignment at (k = 0) and the energy dissipation is zero throughout direct electronic transitions30. The values of computed band gaps for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3},InSr{H}_{3},InBa{H}_{3},$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.113eV,0.427eV,0.400eV,$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.276eV$$\end{document} along with high symmetry points R respectively. The materials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InMg{H}_{3}$$\end{document} possess metallic nature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$($$\end{document}as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{g}=0)$$\end{document} while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3},InSr{H}_{3},InBa{H}_{3},$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} are narrow gap semiconductors of less photo-excited energy loss from photon scattering54. Alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3},InSr{H}_{3},InBa{H}_{3},$$\end{document}and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document}have direct nature of bandgaps33. GGA-PBE methods tend to underestimate bandgaps in semiconductors and insulators. They also have trouble capturing ground state properties in systems with strong electron correlation. This is why the HSE06 hybrid functional was used to compute more accurate bandgap values for the hydride perovskites. Hybrid functional HSE06 guessed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InMg{H}_{3}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document} are metallic while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InCa{H}_{3}\left(0.4574eV\right),$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InSr{H}_{3}\left(0.496eV\right),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBa{H}_{3}\left(1.045eV\right)$$\end{document} are obtained55,56. The HSE06 obtained band structure and DOS are shown in Fig. 5A–F.

Understanding a material’s electronic, optical, and mechanical features and the energy configuration over all regions, requires knowledge of its density of states (DOS)30. The density of states (DOS) is defined as the availability of electronic energy levels per unit energy range and can be calculated theoretically using this equation 15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\left(E\right)=\frac{d{\Omega}\left(\mathrm{E}\right)}{dE}$$\end{document}

Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d{\Omega}$$\end{document} indicates that possibility of all available states and is expressed as16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\left(E\right)=\sum\int\frac{{d}^{3}kx\delta(E-{\epsilon}_{n}(k\left)\right)}{{\left(2\pi\right)}^{3}}$$\end{document}

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{n}\left(k\right)$$\end{document} designates energy eigenvalues57. To further analyze the bandgap outline, investigations are being conducted to explore DOS. DOS also provides a comprehensive view into the qualitative descriptions of VB and CB, together with the accessible energy states. The DOS conveys information about available electronic energy states for band creation. As illustrated in Fig. 5A–F, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} DOS estimated by GGA-PBE exhibit growth in DOS above the fermi level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{F}=0$$\end{document} 57,58. The observed peak values for DOS are given in Table 2. HSE06 Hybrid functional estimated DOS showed peaks at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{F}=0$$\end{document} for Be, Mg, and Ra-based hydrides are non-zero while for other Ca, Sr, and Ba-based possess ~ 0 for DOS at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{F}=0$$\end{document}. For peak values of DOS along with energy values estimated by hybrid HSE06 functional for above and below the fermi level are represented in Table 2.

GGA-PBE and Hybrid HSE06 observed peak values for Density Of States against various energy values for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

The Partial Density of States (PDOS) describes electronic states that are responsible for the creation of band structures and are ordered and controlled by several atomic orbitals. Exploiting GGA-PBE functional PDOS are assessed within energy range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-15eV$$\end{document} to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$15eV$$\end{document} for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} are displayed in Fig. 6A–F 1,57. As elaborated, the VB and CB are formed by s(sky blue), p(orange), d(pink) and f(blue) orbitals for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}. For every hydride, PDOS showed that VB and CB are composed of overall dominant s and p-states for Be, Mg-based hydrides while, most of VB and CB are comprised of s, p, and d-states for Ca, Sr, Ba, and Ra-based hydrides. But for Hybrid HSE06 estimated PDOS showed in Fig. 7, s-sates are dominant in CB and p-states in VB while, d-states become prominent in Ca, Sr, Ba and Ra-based hydrides54.

Fig. 6GGA-PBE functional estimated PDOS for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

GGA-PBE functional estimated PDOS for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

As shown in Fig. 6A–F, and 7A–F, various peak values are obtained by GGA-PBE and HSE06 formulation for s, p, and d-states above and below the fermi level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{F}=0$$\end{document}.

Optical properties

Studying the optical characteristics of a substance can provide insights into its light matter interactions. The optical properties of a substance are determined by how light photons interact with electrons in atomic shells. Photons disintegrate by passing energy to electrons in the lattice structure. These electrons then move into the conduction band. Phonons are formed when photon energy enhances lattice vibrations. Electrons absorb energy in the energy band gap and enter the CB with varying kinetic energy, resulting in indirect band gap semiconductors. Optoelectronic devices de-excite electrons from the CB to the VB, whereas solar cells absorb light. The frequency response of optical properties to incident photon energy is determined by their optical characteristics. Understanding the optical properties of materials is required for optoelectronic applications33,59. The current study calculates optical parameters such as absorption (A), energy loss function (L), reflectivity (R), real and imaginary parts of conductivity, dielectric function, and refractive index for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document}.

Light matter interactions reveal knowledge concerning the optical characteristics of the whole material. As the above mentioned optical characteristics are most effective in the energy regime of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0-15eV$$\end{document} therefore, the optical feartures are studied up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$15eV$$\end{document} of photon energy. The complex dielectric function serves as the foundation for optical properties gained from light-matter interactions, which were computed using mathematical formulas33,60,61. The dielectric function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} represents the material’s sensitivity to light when it falls onto the surface, which is the most significant effect of light matter interaction. It is represented in mathematical form as the sum of real \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({\epsilon}_{1}\right)$$\end{document} and imaginary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({\epsilon}_{2}\right)$$\end{document} parts:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon\left(\omega\right)={\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)+i{\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)=1+\frac{2}{\pi}P\underset{0}{\overset{\infty}{\int}}\frac{{{\omega}^{{\prime}}\epsilon}_{2}\left({\omega}^{{\prime}}\right)}{{{\omega}^{{\prime}}}^{2}-{\omega}^{2}}d\omega$$\end{document}19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varepsilon _2}\left( \omega \right) = \frac{{{e^2}\hbar \:}}{{\pi {m^2}{\omega ^2}}}\sum\limits_{v,c} {\smallint _{BZ}^\infty } {\left| {{M_{cv}}\left( k \right)} \right|^2}\delta \left[ {{\omega _{cv}}\left( k \right) - \omega } \right]{d^3}k$$\end{document}

The above relations (18 and 19) are Kramer–Kroning equations in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} specifies to what degree the substance can disperse, transfer, and reflect light energy while, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} designates conservation as well as dispersal of light energy62. Refractive index, reflectivity, conductivity, and absorption, among all other optical properties are governed by dielectric functions both real \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}and imaginary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} parts. The light matter interactions and the optical as well as electronic structure of a substance are revealed by dielectric functions. All optical attributes have been determined using the GGA-PBE exchange correlation functional61.

The absorption coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} determines how much light energy is absorbed when it strikes a material’s surface. The absorption coefficient determines how much electromagnetic radiation a substance can absorb and is critical when determining how different mediums attenuate light and energy transfer. The absorption coefficient, which contains factors from both real and imaginary sections of the dielectric function, is represented as61,62:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha\left(\omega\right)=\sqrt{2}\omega{\left[\sqrt{{{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)}^{2}+{{\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)}^{2}}-{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)\right]}^{1/2}$$\end{document}

The reflectivity of a substance is the degree to which it can reflect incident light. It represents the effect of incoming photon interaction on a material’s electronic band structure and can be determined using the imaginary component of the dielectric function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} in accordance with the following relation33,63:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\left(\omega\right)={\left|\frac{\sqrt{{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)+{i\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)}-1}{\sqrt{{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)+{i\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)}+1}\right|}^{2}$$\end{document}

The parameter that governs the phenomennon of transportaion of charges at optical frequencies is said to as optical conductivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}. The only phenomennon that governs the mechanistics of emission as well as conduction of photoelectrons is known as photoelectric effect. The computational approachs exploits the following formulation for optical conductivity57,62,63.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma\left(\omega\right)={\sigma}_{1}\left(\omega\right)+i{\sigma}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma\left(\omega\right)={-i\frac{\omega}{4\pi}[{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)+i\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)-1]$$\end{document}

Equations 22 and 23 are the representation of optical conductivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} where it has been shown that it is comprised of two parts one is the real \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sigma}_{1}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} and the other is the imaginary part\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\sigma}_{2}\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}.

The factor governing the degree of bending the light as light passes through the certain material said to as refractive index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} and mathematically can be written as:24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\left(\omega\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{{{(\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right))}^{2}+{{(\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right))}^{2}}}{2}+\frac{{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)}{2}}$$\end{document}25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\left(\omega\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{{{(\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right))}^{2}+{{(\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right))}^{2}}}{2}-\frac{{\epsilon}_{1}\left(\omega\right)}{2}}$$\end{document}

The refractive index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} is comprised of two parts real and imaginary parts and the real part administrates the fact to what an extent a material is transmitting light and it is the ratio of speed of light in vacuum to the speed of light in medium62,64. While The imaginary part\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}also known as extinction cefficient determines the intensity of absorption or attenaution of light energy by a material under considration64. Loss of energy is taken place during light matter interactions and is governed by Energy Loss Function ELF. The phenomenon of scattering and dispersion caused to loss the energy of electrons as a result of transitions. The loss function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document}determines extent of energy loss. There is a direct relation between possibility to scatter and the loss function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} throughout the inner shell transitions. The loss function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\left(\omega\right)$$\end{document} can be calculated by the following relation63,65:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\left(\omega\right)=Img\left(\frac{-1}{\epsilon\left(\omega\right)}\right)=\frac{{\epsilon}_{2}\left(\omega\right)}{{\epsilon}_{1}^{2}\left(\omega\right)+{i\epsilon}_{2}^{2}\left(\omega\right)}$$\end{document}

The observed peak values of all the optical properties in the following Fig. 8A–I, are represented in the Table 4.
