Potentiallity for hydrogen storage

The development of lightweight materials with acceptable gravimetric storage capacities under circumstances of 1–10 bar and 298–473 K is a key problem in using hydrogen as a fuel and for off-board storage applications19,33. Perovskite hydrides are suitable for hydrogen storage applications due to their high gravimetric hydrogen storage capacity and negative formation energies1. The formation energies of compounds play a vital role in judging whether the materials are energetically stable or unstable and are calculated by the following relation:27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varDelta E}_{f}={E}_{total}\left(InX{H}_{3}\right)-[E\left(In\right)+E\left(X\right)+3E\left(H\right)]$$\end{document}

Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{total}\left(InRa{H}_{3}\right)$$\end{document} represents total energy for all alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\left(In\right),E\left(X\right),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3E\left(H\right)$$\end{document} indicate individual atomic ground state energies for\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$In,(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document} respectively. All formation enthalpies are tabulated in Table 2, and as we substituted Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba, and Ra atoms individually, the ∆Ef(keV/atom) became more positive till \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InRaH_3$$\end{document}. The more the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varDelta E}_{f}(keV/atom)$$\end{document} negative the high the material will be thermodynamically stable and easy to synthesize. The stability of order is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InMg{H}_{3}>InRa{H}_{3}>InBa{H}_{3}>InCa{H}_{3}>InSr{H}_{3}>InBe{H}_{3}$$\end{document}. The ratio of stored molar mass hydrogen to host atoms is known as gravimetric storage. To put it another way, the quantity of hydrogen that is kept in a substance is known as gravimetric storage of hydrogen. The two main determinants of hydrogen storage are the disruption temperature of hydrogen and the gravimetric hydrogen capacity10. The % gravimetric hydrogen storage capacity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{wt\%}$$\end{document} for alkaline earth metal-based hydride perovskites \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra)$$\end{document} can be calculated via following expression:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{wt\%}=\left[\frac{\frac{H}{M}{m}_{H}}{{M}_{Host}+\left(\frac{H}{M}{m}_{H}\right)}\times100\right]$$\end{document}

Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{H}{M}$$\end{document} is the hydrogen to corresponding material’s atom ratio, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${m}_{H}$$\end{document} is the mass of host compound. As illustrated in Table 2, the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{wt\%}$$\end{document} for perovskite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}>InMg{H}_{3}>InCa{H}_{3}>InSr{H}_{3}>InBa{H}_{3}>InRa{H}_{3}$$\end{document}. This means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}$$\end{document} can store highest % of\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H}_{2}$$\end{document}. The term volumetric hydrogen storage capacity implies that the amount of hydrogen that may be stored per unit volume and is the following:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\rho}_{vol}=\frac{{{N}_{H}m}_{H}}{V\left(L\right).{N}_{A}}$$\end{document}

Calculated % hydrogen storage by weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{wt\%}$$\end{document} and volumetric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_2$$\end{document} storage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g/L)$$\end{document}for alkaline earth metals based cubic \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InX{H}_{3}\left(X=Be,Mg,Ca,Sr,Ba,Ra\right)$$\end{document}perovskites are elaborated.

Here,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N}_{H}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${m}_{H}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\left(L\right),$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N}_{A}$$\end{document} are number of absorbed hydrogen molecules, mass of H2 molecules, volume of H2 absorber material, and Avogadro’s number respectively. Like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C}_{wt\%},{\rho}_{vol}$$\end{document} also possess same order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBe{H}_{3}>InMg{H}_{3}>InCa{H}_{3}>InSr{H}_{3}>InBa{H}_{3}>InRa{H}_{3}.$$\end{document}.

The calculated gravimetric hydrogen storage capacity of InXH3 is 2.38 wt%, which is substantially lower than the U.S. Department of Energy 2025 system-level target of 5.5 wt% for onboard hydrogen storage66. This value represents only approximately 43% of the DOE benchmark and therefore does not satisfy vehicular storage requirements. Similar limitations have been reported for several ABH₃-type hydride perovskites, where intrinsic hydrogen content is structurally fixed and difficult to enhance without chemical modification13,18. Consequently, the present InXH₃ compounds should be viewed as model systems for understanding structure–stability–bonding relationships in hydride perovskites rather than immediate candidates for mobile hydrogen storage. Achieving DOE-level performance would require destabilization strategies, partial substitution, or composite formation to increase usable hydrogen capacity and optimize thermodynamics50.

A hydrogen storage material needs to store enough hydrogen and release it at a temperature that fits the intended system. The desorption temperature is an important measure of whether a material is practical for a given application. The Gibbs relation provides a way to estimate this temperature44,67.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varDelta G=\varDelta H-{T}_{des}.\varDelta S$$\end{document}

The value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}$$\end{document}can be obtained from the equation under normal temperature and pressure. At standard conditions, the Gibbs free energy change is zero \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\varDelta G=0)$$\end{document}, so the relation simplifies and allows direct evaluation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}$$\end{document}.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}=\frac{\varDelta H}{\varDelta S}$$\end{document}

The hydride production enthalpy, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varDelta H,$$\end{document} and the entropy change associated with hydrogen release, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varDelta S$$\end{document}, are often estimated using a standard entropy value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-130.7J{mol}^{-1}{K}^{-1}$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_2$$\end{document} under typical conditions. In applied settings, materials with desorption temperatures between 233 and 333 K are often considered suitable. The estimated values for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}\left(K\right)$$\end{document} and hydride production enthalpy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varDelta H$$\end{document} are given in Table 5. As the order for the estimated values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}\left(K\right)$$\end{document} is given as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBeH_3<InSrH_3<InCaH_3<InBaH_3<InRaH_3<InMgH_3$$\end{document}. This suggest that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InBeH_3$$\end{document} takes much less temperature as compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$InMgH_3$$\end{document}to give off \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_2$$\end{document} molecule. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T}_{des}\left(K\right)$$\end{document} for all hydrides is greater than room temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\sim300K)$$\end{document}, this makes them excellent candidates for off board hydrogen storage applications27. Phonon dispersion calculations were carried out to evaluate the dynamic stability of the optimized cubic phases. The phonon spectra showed imaginary modes at high symmetry points X, M, G, R, indicating dynamic instability within the harmonic approximation at 0 K. Similar soft mode behavior has been reported in hydride and oxyhydride perovskites and is commonly linked to symmetry lowering distortions or anion ordering rather than structural collapse20,22. In perovskite systems, imaginary modes often signal possible phase transitions or stabilization through anharmonic effects at finite temperature68. Importantly, all investigated compounds satisfy the Born-Huang mechanical stability criteria, confirming their elastic stability. Finite temperature stabilization through anharmonic lattice vibrations is well recognized in perovskite type materials68. Although ab initio molecular dynamics simulations would provide further insight into thermal stability, such simulations require large supercells and long simulation times for hydride systems due to the presence of light hydrogen atoms. These calculations are beyond the current computational resources and are therefore proposed for future investigation.

Conclusion

In this work, a comprehensive first principles investigation of cubic InXH3 (X = Be, Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba, Ra) hydride perovskites was performed with primary focus on their solid state hydrogen storage applicability. Structural optimization confirmed that all compounds stabilized in the cubic Pm-3 m phase, with Goldschmidt tolerance factors ranging from 0.8450 to 1.0884, demonstrating geometric compatibility and intrinsic structural stability required for hydrogen hosting. Mechanical stability was verified through elastic constants satisfying the Born-Huang criteria. A systematic reduction in lattice rigidity was observed across the series, with bulk modulus decreasing from 25.55 GPa for InMgH3 to 14.09 GPa for InRaH3. The increase in Poisson’s ratio from 0.22 to 0.3374 indicated enhanced ionic bonding character toward heavier alkaline earth compositions. These trends highlighted tunable mechanical behavior that could influence hydrogen diffusion and release characteristics. Electronic structure analysis revealed metallic behavior for InBeH3 and InMgH3, while InCaH3, InSrH3, and InBaH3 exhibited direct band gap semiconducting nature. Hybrid HSE06 calculations refined the band gaps to 0.574 eV for InCaH3, 0.496 eV for InSrH3, and 1.045 eV for InBaH3, confirming stable electronic environments. Optical response evaluated within 0 to 35 eV showed strong dielectric activity and absorption features, indicating structural robustness under photon irradiation. Hydrogen storage assessment demonstrated negative formation energies for all compositions, confirming thermodynamic feasibility and synthesizability. Gravimetric and volumetric hydrogen storage capacities followed a decreasing trend with increasing atomic mass. InBeH3 exhibited the highest gravimetric capacity of 2.38 wt% and volumetric capacity of 57.15gH2/L, making it the most promising candidate within the series. Estimated desorption temperatures were above room temperature and increased systematically from lighter to heavier elements, suggesting controlled hydrogen release suitable for off board storage systems. Overall, the results established lightweight alkaline earth based hydride perovskites, particularly InBeH3, as promising solid state hydrogen storage materials and provided a clear compositional design strategy for developing stable, tunable, and thermodynamically viable hydride systems for future hydrogen energy technologies.
